## HERMAN FICTULD. <note>Herman Fictuld was a pseudonym for a prominent 18th-century German alchemist and leader of the Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for an alchemical treatise identifying "Azoth" and "Fire" as the fundamental components of the Philosophers' Stone. The author, Herman Fictuld, uses biblical justification to link alchemical creation with the divine creation of the world.</summary>

->to the Devotees<-

To lay out things that are grounded in Nature; therefore, we have not wished to lead and guide you through crooked paths, but rather along the straight path according to the first <term>Subjectum</term> <note>original: "Subjecto." In alchemical literature, the "Subject" refers to the raw material or "Prime Matter" (materia prima) required to begin the Great Work.</note> and the seed of the same. This path is prayer and true repentance, by which to reconcile **GOD**, who has cast a darkness over our hearts and eyes, and to obtain from Him by grace that the darkness be taken from us and we be made to see again—to see that which the natural man, in his unrepentant way of life, is unable to see. Now, when this darkness is cast off from us, we see through the true light the fundamental cause of why we previously, while we still walked in unrepentance, could not grasp these things. For we were, as the dear Savior himself testifies, blind and did not see the light. Nevertheless, we did not wish to be blind, but believed that we were seeing; yet we did not know that man has a double birth: one according to the flesh and one according to the spirit. Likewise, truly, truly I say to you, unless someone is born of water and Spirit, he cannot enter into the **Kingdom of GOD**. Christ testifies to this with a double oath when He says, "Truly, truly," as if He wanted to say: it is certainly so, therefore take good heed. Now I would also like to ask: if you believe that you are not blind, but are true children of **GOD**, then when did this same <term>rebirth</term> <note>German: "Wiedergeburt." For it follows from your way of life that you know nothing of the new birth, or else have made yourselves incapable of it. And therefore, because your works are done according to the outer man, they are works of darkness which have no fellowship with the light; for this reason, you are also blind. In alchemical thought, this describes the fundamental change from a base, worldly state to a divine, spiritual state—mirroring the process of turning lead into gold.</note>, and becomes a Man of **GOD**, who then can understand the things that are of the Spirit of **GOD**, and thus also the things that **GOD** the Lord originally formed and placed into Nature.

For alchemists like Fictuld, this was not just a material substance for making gold, but a divine mystery representing the perfection of both matter and spirit.</note>, which we have resolved to discuss in the clearest and briefest manner. # Preface.

to mock them, and that for political reasons; on one hand, to embolden the possessors of the <term>Hermetic Arcana</term> <note>original: "Hermetischen Arcanen." These are the "Hermetic Secrets"—the hidden, sacred knowledge of alchemy traditionally attributed to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, as if they would, upon this challenge, immediately appear on the field of battle. This is a desire more laughable than irritating, especially if one fails to reflect upon their motives, and so forth. On the other hand, [they do this] to display their supposed cleverness to the world, as if speaking an untruth in jest were no sin. Such was the case with a small treatise titled *The Non-Existent Philosopher's Stone* <note>original: "Lapis Philosophorum Nonens"</note>, asserting that the <term>Stone of the Wise</term> <note>Also known as the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting long life.</note> never existed and does not exist even now, presented for reflection through divine and human wisdom by Christoph Pflugk, a pastor in Vielau, printed in the year 1732.

Likewise, there is the treatise *The Foolish Adept* <note>original: "Adeptus Ineptus"</note>, or the Discovery of the Falsely Renowned Art Called Alchemy, in which the vanity of such an art is clearly demonstrated, the principles of the alchemists are examined, their deceit is exposed, and the impossibility of <term>metal transformation</term> <note>Known as transmutation; the central alchemical process of changing one element into another.</note> is at least

<summary>Fictuld identifies two types of critics: those who mock alchemists to bait them into revealing secrets and those who write skeptical treatises to appear intellectually superior. He specifically cites works by Christoph Pflugk and others that dismiss the Philosopher's Stone as a myth.</summary>

<keywords>Hermetic Arcana, Philosopher's Stone, Christoph Pflugk, Adeptus Ineptus, alchemy critics, metal transformation, skepticism, 18th-century alchemy</keywords>

# Preface.

demonstrated as most probable; as well as the <term>universal medicine</term> <note>Also known as the Panacea, a legendary substance believed by alchemists to cure all diseases and prolong life.</note> and other alleged alchemical feats are discussed, published by **TARSANDER**, or Master Georg Wilhelm Wegner, Pastor at Germendorf and Roßheim, in the year 1744.

And we ought, by rights, to refute these follies and slanders, and show how erroneous, perverse, false, and unfounded their claims are. However, since so many writings already exist by true possessors of the Stone of the Wise, as well as others who were eyewitnesses to the many <term>projections</term> <note>original: "projectionem." The final stage of the alchemical process where the Philosopher's Stone is "projected" or cast onto molten base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</note> and metal transformations that have occurred in Europe for many centuries, these dark gentlemen shall not be worthy of any further refutation.

But since we have directed our present work toward the <term>Subject of Water</term> <note>original: "Subjectum des Wassers." In alchemy, this rarely refers to common water, but rather the "Philosophical Water" or "Mercurial Water"—the foundational substance or "First Matter" used to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, and have proven in our thesis <note>original: "in Thesi."</note> that this is the one and only thing of which the Wise speak with such high praise,

<summary>The author dismisses the skeptical writings of Georg Wilhelm Wegner (Tarsander), arguing that the long history of successful alchemical transmutations makes further refutation of critics unnecessary. He then introduces his primary topic: "the Subject of Water" as the essential matter of alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>Georg Wilhelm Wegner, Tarsander, universal medicine, refutation of critics, projections, metal transformation, Subject of Water, philosophical water</keywords>

# Preface.

it cannot appear as anything other than wondrous to many, just as it is in truth wondrous in all its parts and variations. For wondrous is the high art of *Alchemy* <note>original: "Alchimia."</note>, wondrous is the *First Matter* <note>original: "prima Materia." In alchemical philosophy, this is the formless, primordial substance required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, that one thing, the hidden *Subject* <note>original: "Subjectum." The underlying material or "topic" of the chemical work.</note> which we here call Water; wondrous is the origin of this Water; indeed, wondrous is GOD, as the origin of all things; therefore, our manner of writing must also be wondrous, yet to the honor and praise of GOD.

We have stated in this work that Water originated from GOD, but that the Water, by means of heat, entered into *Putrefaction* <note>original: "Putrefaction." The stage of decay or fermentation in the alchemical process, often associated with the color black (the nigredo), which was believed to be necessary for the purification of matter.</note>, became muddy, and from this Water the entire world originated and all creatures were brought forth. Now, an objection could be raised against us, since we have proven from Scripture and Nature that GOD is a most holy and pure GOD, who is separated from all creatures and higher than the heavens themselves, so that before His purity and

<summary>The author describes the "wondrous" nature of alchemy and the divine origin of the "First Matter," which he identifies as a primordial Water. He explains that through heat and decay (putrefaction), this divine water became the physical foundation of the world and all living beings.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, First Matter, Putrefaction, Divine Origin, Creation, Philosophical Water, Nature and Scripture</keywords>

# Preface.

holiness nothing can stand; even the heavens are not pure before Him. Yet GOD is the origin of the Water, the Water is the seed of all nature, and the entire world has come from that same source; and yet the world and its creatures are impure. From whence then did this impurity originate? To those, this serves as an answer: From the Water alone. 

Thus they might further reproach us: how could it come from the Water, since it has its origin in GOD? To counter them, it is necessary to show that the Water originated and went forth from GOD pure and holy, but only afterward—by the most holy ordinance of GOD—entered into *Putrefaction* <note>original: "Putrefaction." In alchemical terms, this refers to a stage of breaking down or "rotting" a substance to its core components so it can be reborn or purified.</note> through a heat working within itself. This is the means by which the Water allowed itself to be divided into its elements, especially since without *Putrefaction* no perfect *Separation* <note>original: "Separation." The process of isolating the various elements or "essences" of a substance, which was considered impossible without first breaking the material down through decay.</note> can occur.

There is no doubt, however, that you will further reproach us, saying that no thing enters into *Putrefaction* unless it already possesses impurities within it. But we answer that you are mistaken, and understand neither the Scripture

<summary>The author explains that while Water is divinely pure in its origin, God ordained that it undergo "putrefaction" (decay) through internal heat. This process of breaking down is presented as the necessary mechanism that allowed the primordial Water to separate into the various elements and creatures of the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>divine origin, Putrefaction, Separation, elements of nature, purity of God, seed of nature, alchemy, creation theory</keywords>

Finally, we have one more word to speak with you, lovers of Hermetic Wisdom and Truth; and specifically, for the first part: because we call this a high truth—as indeed it is—our intention, will, and opinion are by no means to seek to make such a high art popular and pleasant, or to recruit and encourage more lovers for it to seek and investigate it; no, not at all. Rather, we protest against such a thing—namely, that the high art, <term>Alchemy</term> <note>The art of transformation, both of metals and of the human soul.</note>, is not every man’s business, and thus not everyone should occupy themselves with researching it. 

Although there are many <term>authors</term> <note>original Latin: "Autores."</note> who praise and exalt Alchemy quite excessively, claiming it is so trivial that one need only reach out, and just as one draws water with a vessel, so one can draw gold—a procedure that is not to be praised, as it has made many people reckless, who [have thrown] their <term>livelihood</term> <note>original: "Nahrungs-Beruf," referring to the trade or profession by which a person earns their daily bread.</note> to the wall

# Preface.

[if you] are deceived by such **vagabonds** <note>original: "Landstreichern." Refers to the itinerant swindlers mentioned on the previous page.</note>, then you must take the blame upon yourselves. In the future, leave the true **alchemical writers** and **adepts** <term>Adepts</term> <note>original: "Adeptos." Derived from the Latin *adeptus* ("one who has attained"); a title for those who have successfully discovered the Philosopher's Stone.</note>—as possessors of **Hermetic secrets** <term>Hermetic Arcanum</term> <note>original: "Hermetischer Arcanen." From the Latin *arcanum* (secret/mystery); refers to the hidden, sacred knowledge of alchemy.</note>—unaccused. You always seek to blame them, as if they were responsible for such misfortunes, arguing that because they (to preserve the truth) did not describe the process **plainly enough** <note>original: "nicht deutsch genug." Literally "not German enough," a common idiom of the period meaning "not clearly" or "not in plain language."</note>, you were thereby led astray.

Therefore, be warned, and close your ears, heart, and **purse** <note>original: "Geld-Beutel."</note> against such **siren-song** <note>A mythological reference to the Sirens, whose enchanting music lured sailors to their doom; here it represents the seductive but false promises of gold-makers.</note>, which is capable of lulling you to sleep. It leaves you behind with a sorrowful backward glance and heavy sighs, while you live under the constant hope of **mountains of gold** and **castles in the air** <note>original: "in der Lufft daher fliegenden Gold-Berge und Schlösser." Literally "mountains of gold and castles flying in the air."</note>.

Therefore, accept our benevolent advice as a warning and with thanks. Recognize that our goodwill toward you, and our aversion toward these destructive **swindlers** <note>original: "Land-Betrüger."</note>, has moved us to this end; especially since we frankly confess that we are not indeed embittered or incensed against the persons themselves, but rather against their **evil and perverted works**...

# Preface.

[has been] brought about <note>Continuing from the previous page: "...[the disgrace] has been brought about..."</note>, not because they have cheated us so much, but because, through their **deceit**, they have brought the true **Hermetic Philosophy** <term>Hermetic Philosophy</term> <note>A tradition of knowledge named after Hermes Trismegistus, combining alchemy, astrology, and theurgy to understand the relationship between the divine and the material world.</note> into such ill repute and disrepute. Indeed, they have so disgraced it that many people are ashamed even to pick up a book on the subject, let alone to attempt any practical work with their own hands, for fear that they will be despised and mocked by others and become the subject of their ridicule <note>original: "ihr Liedlein werden," literally "become their little song," an idiom meaning to be the target of mockery.</note>.

This is a great pity, for in former times, it was considered an honor and a joy to be known by this name—as one who is not lazy or idle, but who strives to explore noble **Nature** and the **secrets of God** which He has placed within it. Nevertheless, one should not cease to seek and explore the **Truth** simply because of these fraudsters; for Truth remains Truth into eternity, and for that very reason, we recommend it most highly.

Moreover, we recommend to you this little book of ours, ***AZOTH & FIRE*** <note>original: "AZOTH & IGNIS." Azoth is a term for the "universal medicine" or soul of the world; Ignis is Latin for fire, the primary tool of the alchemist.</note>.

### The First Book of Moses, Chapter 1 <note>Commonly known as the Book of Genesis.</note>

In the beginning God created the heavens and the earth.

### The First Book of Moses, Chapter 1

1. In the beginning, God created the heavens and the earth.

2. And the earth was waste and empty, and it was dark upon the deep <note>original: "Abgrund"; referring to the primordial abyss or the vast depths of the unformed world.</note>, and the Spirit of God was hovering above the waters.

4. And God saw the light, that it was good; then God made a distinction <note>original: "Unterscheidung"; throughout this section, the author emphasizes the active separation or "distinction" of primordial elements.</note> between the light and the darkness.

7. Then God made the firmament, and made a distinction between the water that was under the firmament and the water that was above the firmament; and it was so:

# Of the Element of Water
### What it is, from whence it originated, what its powers are, and to what it is dedicated, treated in this short treatise.

...and been recast; or whether such
things here took their first primordial
beginning? Of this, no sufficient
testimonies or signs are available.
It is enough, however, that the text says:
**God** created—that is, **God** formed a
mass, lump, or <term>Chaos</term> <note>The formless, dark state of matter before it was ordered by divine command.</note>,
from which thereafter the seven magnificent
<term>Days' Works</term> <note>Refers to the biblical sequence of creation in Genesis.</note>
followed and flowed.

However, ignorant people might
ask: if nothing was present, how
could **God** then create everything out
of nothing? To those we answer
that they should consider, firstly, what
the Evangelist **John** says in the 1st
Chapter: "In the beginning was the Word, and
the Word was with **God**, and **God**
was the Word; the same was in the
beginning with **God**. All things are made through
the same, and without the same
nothing is made that is made."
Likewise **David**, King and Prophet, in the

For water has this quality within itself: it moistens, softens, and makes that which is dead as if it were alive; indeed, it refreshes and quickens it, so that it may be freed from ruin and death, and conversely applies its power toward the production of fruits. Therefore, excessive dryness and excessive wetness are both harmful to the soil. For while the heat of the sun indeed builds up the land, if it persists for too long, the land becomes completely parched and unfit for the further growth of fruits. ### Of the **Azoth** <term>Azoth: Regarded by alchemists as the "Universal Solvent" or the essential "mercury" of all metals; the beginning and end of the transformation process.</term> and **Fire** <note>original: "Ignis".</note>.

...with the **Great Flood** <note>original: "Sündfluth". The author uses the biblical deluge as a metaphor for the "dissolution" of matter in the alchemist's flask.</note>, he had sent forth a raven, which however did not return; from this the **Patriarch** <note>Noah.</note> could conclude that the water had dried up and the raven had found nourishment. For this reason, the first gate has been depicted as a black field, as a sign that the world after the Great Flood was filled with dead bodies and carrion.

For this reason the **Sages** say: Cut off the **Raven’s head**, make the black white, and the white red; then you have what you seek. For you must know that the redness as well as the whiteness, and the whiteness as well as the redness, proceeds from the **blackness** <note>In alchemy, the "nigredo" or black stage is the state of putrefaction where matter is broken down so that it may be reborn in a purified state.</note>. Therefore, a certain author has written very well of this, saying: That the **nymphs** had pulled the chariot of **Venus** <note>Venus represents the "green" or "red" stages of alchemy, often associated with copper and the growth of the soul.</note> as well as the **doves**, and these had pulled the chariot of **Diana** <note>Diana, the moon goddess, represents the "albedo" or white stage, associated with silver and purification.</note> just as well as that of Venus. With this, we believe enough has been said to the **Children of Wisdom** <term>Children of Wisdom: A term for fellow alchemists or those initiated into the secret meanings of the art.</term>.

### Of **Azoth** <term>Azoth: The "Universal Solvent" in alchemy, considered the essential agent of transformation that contains both the beginning and the end of the Great Work.</term> and **Fire** <note>original: "Ignis".</note>

...of a former joint origin, since they originated and were born from one blood, they unite with one another again, combined, incorporated, and shared in common, as if they had never been separated from each other; however, in a completely different form and figure, and indeed in a twofold sense. 

For firstly in that, as the mixing of heaven with the earth, of the upper with the lower <note>A reference to the famous Hermetic maxim from the Emerald Tablet: "That which is below is like that which is above."</note>, of the volatile with the fixed, of the hot with the cold, of the male seed with the female, and of the moist with the dry: and then secondly, **Mercury** <term>Mercury: Not just the liquid metal, but the "philosophical" principle of fluidity, spirit, and transformation.</term> with **Sulfur** <term>Sulfur: The alchemical principle of soul, heat, and combustibility.</term>, silver with gold, the King with the Queen, and the bride with the bridegroom. 

Which then together constitute a single, eternal and indestructible being, which cannot be separated by any element, but is eternally remaining, which is also prefigured by the snake, which bites its own tail, or devours the same <note>This refers to the **Ouroboros**, an ancient symbol of eternity and the cyclical nature of the alchemical work, where the end returns to the beginning.</note>; that is, the end has found its origin.

### Of **Azoth** <term>Azoth: To the alchemist, this was the "Universal Solvent" or the essential spirit of life that contains both the beginning and the end of all things.</term> and **Fire** <note>original: "Ignis".</note>.

H 2

Thus, we have now sufficiently spoken and treated of those waters of the upper and lower regions—namely, that these are the **Azoth** and **Fire**, the **First Matter** <term>First Matter: Latin "Prima Materia"; the formless, primordial substance from which all things are created and to which the alchemist must return a substance before it can be transformed.</term>, the **Philosophers' Stone** <term>Philosophers' Stone: Latin "Lapis Philosophorum"; the legendary goal of alchemy, representing perfection, enlightenment, and the power to transmute base metals into gold.</term>, and the true elemental water and fire of the sages. From these, the whole world, along with all creatures and creations, originated, and from them they still daily sprout and grow. We do not doubt that in the eyes and ears of the learned, this will seem far too foolish and distasteful, because it is not composed or adorned with scholarly or official chancery <note>The author refers to "Kanzleisprache," the formal, overly complex legalistic language used in government offices of the time.</note> words; nevertheless, we flatter ourselves that such will find its purpose and utility among the **Sons of Wisdom** <note>A traditional term for those initiated into the secrets of alchemy.</note>.

In this connection, the learned should know that it is always a greater thing to invent a matter than to correct or criticize it. ## In Conclusion.

We have wished to append here the beautiful and magnificent prayer of the late blessed **Arndt** <note>Johann Arndt (1555–1621) was a famous Lutheran theologian. His work *True Christianity* was deeply influential and often blended mystical theology with alchemical metaphors of purification.</note> from the sixth chapter of the fourth book of the first part, concerning the **Six Days' Works of God**. We do this partly because of its importance and its great suitability to our work, and partly for its utility in honoring and praising **God**. If space permitted, it would be most useful to include the entire chapter upon which this prayer was formed; the prayer reads as follows:

> Bless the Lord, O my soul! Lord, my **God**, you are very glorious in your works; You vault the heavens above with water / you let water stand above the mountains; <note>A reference to Psalm 104, describing the architecture of creation.</note> And because this **element** <note>original: "Element." In the four-element theory (Earth, Air, Fire, Water), water was seen as the carrier of life-force.</note> is suited to enter into the interior of all bodies, you place within it the seed, nourishment, and growth of all things, so that the earth and all that it holds or bears consists of water or exists in water through your **Word**. This **living water** <term>Living Water: In this context, it refers both to the biblical "Water of Life" and the alchemical "Universal Mercury" or "Azoth" discussed on the previous page, which acts as a spiritual solvent.</term> is full of mystery / yet so lightly esteemed that a foolish person does not believe it, and a fool does not regard it. Water cleanses all things, and without it we would have to <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; the catchword "dassels" indicates the next word is "dasselbe" (the same).</note>

# Preface.
## to the Lovers
### of Chemical Wisdom. <term>Chemical Wisdom: *Chymische Weißheit*, a historical term for Alchemy, emphasizing that it was considered a divine and philosophical science rather than just a precursor to modern chemistry.</term>

<image-desc>A decorative initial 'N' enclosed within an ornate circular frame topped with a crown and supported by a winged base.</image-desc>

Since many have ventured to write about the famous, world-renowned name, **AVREVM VELLVS** <note>original Latin: *AVREVM VELLVS*, meaning the "Golden Fleece." In Greek mythology, this was the fleece of the winged ram Chrysomallos; in alchemy, it is a frequent symbol for the Philosopher's Stone or the completion of the Great Work.</note>, or the **Golden Fleece**, yet none among them has sufficiently explained what the nature of it truly is; instead, when they began and it was incumbent upon them to speak of it, they hesitated, broke off, and remained silent. This served no one except to double their desires and increase their searching. Thus, we have also <meta>The sentence ends abruptly here due to the page break. The catchword "gut" suggests the next page begins with "gut befunden" (found it good/fitting) or a similar phrase.</meta>

<summary>This preface introduces the author's critique of previous alchemical writers who have mentioned the "Golden Fleece" without explaining its true nature, promising a more transparent account for seekers of wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Preface, Chemical Wisdom, Aureum Vellus, Golden Fleece, Alchemy, Hermetic Philosophy, Herman Fictuld</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page completes the opening sentence of the Preface from the preceding page. The author transitions from justifying his topic—the Golden Fleece—to a "humility topos," a traditional rhetorical device where the author compares his own modest abilities to the great orators of antiquity to emphasize the weight of his subject.</meta>

<meta>ink bleed-through from reverse page</meta>

...deemed it good to deliver a discourse concerning this world-renowned, but nonetheless to many unknown name; of what its true nature consists, how it originated, why the **alchemists** <term>Alchemists: *Alchimisten*, practitioners of the "Art," who sought the transformation of matter and the attainment of the Philosopher's Stone.</term> made use of it, and how it rose to such great prominence.

If in former times those world-famous, highly wise, indeed most learned and eloquent men—such as **Plato**, who was made confused by the clamor of the people; **Cicero**, who brought forth his words with trembling and stammering; **Isocrates** <note>Isocrates (436–338 BC) was a famous Greek rhetorician who, despite his brilliance, was notoriously plagued by stage fright and a weak voice, leading him to focus on written rather than spoken oratory.</note>, who out of fear and dread could not open his mouth; **Socrates**, who knew no words to bring forth at all; and a Greek **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus of Eresus, the successor to Aristotle, who was said to have been struck speechless when appearing before the Council of Areopagus.</note>, who stood there struck dumb: whenever they were to present or propose something to a community. What then should we think, we who possess not the least resemblance of wisdom, science, learning, and eloquence? And what is more: since today’s world far exceeds and surpasses the ancient world in learning, languages, and **eloquence** <term>Eloquence: *Wohlredenheit* or *Beredtsamkeit*, the art of persuasive and beautiful speech, highly valued in both classical and Baroque education.</term>,

<summary>The author explains his intent to clarify the origins and meaning of the "Golden Fleece" in alchemy, while humbly comparing his own oratorical skills to the great, yet sometimes faltering, philosophers of ancient Greece and Rome.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Fleece, alchemy, humility topos, Plato, Cicero, Isocrates, Socrates, Theophrastus, eloquence, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface from page 124. The author concludes his rhetorical display of humility, transitioning from his self-perceived inadequacy to his duty to uncover ancient truths for the benefit of his fellow man, despite potential criticism.</meta>

# Preface

...proceeds; and also, because we speak not only to one congregation or land, but to many successive ages, and set our thoughts free; so that in this contemplation we should not only fall silent, but—like **Erasmus**, **Morienus** <note>Morienus was a legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist, famously said to have taught the secrets of the Philosopher's Stone to the King Khalid ibn Yazid.</note>, and **Archilochus** <note>The Greek poet Archilochus famously admitted to throwing away his shield in battle to save his life, an act of "anti-heroic" honesty that the author here uses to signify his own vulnerability before the world's judgment.</note>—cast our weapons and shields from us, and flee before the terror and fear of the world. 

Inasmuch as such a **History of Antiquities** faces much contradiction and is exposed to powerful **critics**; nevertheless, because we are sufficiently assured by the literal understanding as well as by the **symbolism** <term>Symbolism: *Sinnbilde*, referring to the use of allegorical images or "emblems" to hide spiritual or chemical secrets.</term> and the traditions of the ancients, we have endeavored to seek out the high truth from its foundation, from beneath the old rubble and **ruins** <note>original: *ruderis*, from the Latin *rudera*, meaning the debris or stones of a collapsed building; a metaphor for the forgotten wisdom of the past.</note>, and to present it to those who love the truth: just as the Law and the Gospel bind us to serve our neighbor.

We have nothing at all to do with the high and proud spirits of this world, nor with all their learning, unless it be that, as the Lord spoke there to Shimei: **Curse David** <note>A biblical reference to 2 Samuel 16:10. When Shimei cursed King David, David refused to punish him, suggesting that perhaps God had commanded the insult to test David's humility. The author implies he will endure the insults of "learned" critics with similar stoicism.</note>.

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, the author justifies his work as a service to humanity, aiming to recover "high truth" from ancient ruins despite the inevitable attacks from academic critics. He frames his endurance of such criticism as a religious duty, invoking the biblical patience of King David.</summary>

# Preface

Therefore we give the assurance that we will not respond with even a single **iota** <note>The smallest letter in the Greek alphabet, often used to mean "not the slightest bit."</note>. However, such people should be mindful of the instruction which **King David** delivered to his son **Solomon**, when he says: *You know what evil **Shimei** showed me, and what **Mephibosheth** did to me on the day of my persecution, when I fled from **Absalom**, and I swore to them to do no harm; but you shall deal with them according to your wisdom.* <note>This passage refers to 1 Kings 2:8-9. David advises Solomon on how to handle those who were disloyal during the rebellion of his son Absalom. The mention of Mephibosheth here is curious, as the biblical account usually highlights Shimei and the sons of Barzillai in this context; the author may be conflating various biblical accounts of David's trials.</note> Something similar might also proceed from a faithful Solomon.

Thus, with the present work, we once again present to you—specifically as a **supplement** <note>original: *Nachtrab*, literally a "rearguard" or a following troop; here meaning a follow-up to a previous publication.</note> to our **Touchstone** <term>Touchstone: *Probier-Stein*, a black stone used to test the purity of gold and silver by the color of the streak they leave; metaphorically, a standard for judging the truth of alchemy.</term>—a quite remarkable history under the name ***Aureum Vellus*** <note>Latin for "Golden Fleece."</note> or **Golden Fleece**, and within it, the substance and practice of the **Philosopher’s Stone**. 

We give you the assurance that this is no new report, no fairy tale, no never-before-heard story, nor any fantasy, **Rumor** <note>original: *Fama*, likely referring to the *Fama Fraternitatis*, a famous Rosicrucian manifesto of the era, or simply to public gossip.</note>, or novels; no, not at all, but rather an ancient, historical, high, noble, and precious truth, which is found in the writings of the wise...

Herewith we have intended to show, by way of a preface: Firstly, that this high royal science or art <note>The "Royal Art" is a traditional name for alchemy, suggesting it is the most noble of all pursuits.</note> originated from **God**, the eternal **Creator**, from whom everything has its beginning and origin. There is yet another great folly under the sun that we see: there are people—even highly learned ones—who do not seek the straight path to the **Great Universal** <term>Universal|The 'Great Work' of alchemy, seeking the Philosopher's Stone which can transmute all base metals and heal all diseases, rather than specific, limited results.</term>, but instead wish first to become masters of a **particular** <term>Particulair|Specific alchemical processes that aim to transmute only a small amount of a specific metal or produce a limited effect, often seen as secondary or even fraudulent compared to the Universal Work.</term>. They busy themselves with **cements** <note>Alchemical powders or pastes used in heat treatments to refine or color metals.</note>, with **augmentations**, with **bringing in and out**, with **figurations**, and many other such **breadless arts** <note>original: *Brodlosen Künsten*; a term for pursuits or "sciences" that provide no actual income or sustenance.</note>. Or, as one recently claimed, that he possessed a **perpetual augmentation** <note>original: *Augmentationem perpetuam*. A theoretical process where a small amount of gold or silver could be continuously increased or "grown."</note> and wished to deliver it in exchange for an advance of 1,000 **Reichstalers** <note>Abbreviated in text as Rthl.; the standard silver currency of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>. Such processes, whatever names they may have, if they are outside the **Universal Fountain**, are lies and deceptions whose father is the devil, through which honest people are robbed of their property. For, outside the Universal Fountain, there is no true

# Chapter I.

### Regarding the Authors from whom the History of the Golden Fleece has been drawn.

The Golden Fleece <note>original: *Golden-Vließ* or *Aureum Vellus*</note> was a term and a peculiar figure of speech that is very ancient; it was already known and in use among the oldest eras and peoples. It is found cited among the Sages <note>original: *Magis*, referring to the Magi or wise men of antiquity</note>, Cabalists <note>Students of Jewish mysticism, often adapted by Christian hermeticists</note>, and Poets, as well as by chroniclers, chronologists, historians, and geographers. It is an expression used to signify something extraordinary and worthy of wonder. It is as if it calls out to those passing by, saying: "Dear traveler! Redeem the time <note>A phrase meaning to make the most of one's time, often with a spiritual or studious connotation</note> to stand here a little while and observe me; indeed, rather redeem the time to conduct an investigation into me, and the benefit you shall enjoy from it...

<meta>catchword: will (original: wirst)</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the first chapter, defining the "Golden Fleece" as an ancient and multifaceted symbol recognized by historical, poetic, and mystical authorities alike.</summary>

<keywords>Golden Fleece, Aureum Vellus, Magi, Cabalists, history of alchemy, Herman Fictuld, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the personified address of the Golden Fleece begun on the previous page. The "Fleece" speaks directly to the reader, promising to reveal the secrets of nature and the "sacred bodies" hidden from the uninitiated. It describes the term "Golden Fleece" as a profound symbol pointing toward the First Substance and the practical work of alchemy.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>

...will reward your effort many times over. My outer form will show you what is hidden behind this curtain, and will place you in such wonder as you never expected; indeed, such a light and radiance will seize you, which publicly reveals the treasury of all nature and presents the mystery-filled images of the sacred bodies, which the highest Wisdom <note>Likely referring to Sophia or Divine Wisdom, a central figure in Hermetic philosophy</note> keeps buried and preserved in this vault.

Yes, it is a figure of speech that requires deep and far-reaching reflection, since it flows from a single center, yet possesses two foundations or abysses <note>The author uses *Ab- oder Ungründe*, playing on the idea of a foundation that is also a bottomless depth</note>, which are two extremes whose ends our reason can never grasp: first, regarding what they are in and of themselves, and second, what they denote and signify. This figure of speech is a depth and an abyss of all wisdom; it is the focal point toward the true center of all the wise, concerning both the First Substance <note>original: *Primæ Substantiæ*; the primordial matter required for the alchemical Great Work</note> as well as the Practice of the Stone <note>original: *Praxis des Lapidis*; the actual laboratory process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>.

# Of the Golden Fleece.

<meta>The text begins mid-word, completing "Lapidis" from the previous page's catchword.</meta>

...of the **Stone of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Lapidis Philosophorum*; the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and grant long life</note> itself. Under these terms, the mentioned philosophical writers—who were conscious of the basis and foundation of this way of speaking and possessed the "Main Work"—described and left behind the **art of gold-making**, out of love for posterity, as a testamentary legacy to their sons and disciples of wisdom. 

The others, however, who had no certainty regarding this, but instead judged according to their own speculations and imaginings, saw indeed that something great, secret, and golden was hidden beneath it; yet, what was meant and intended to be said by it remained a veiled work to them. Therefore, they fell upon all sorts of ideas, especially upon **metallurgy** and the mining of rich gold veins; as well as upon a book or scrolls made of ram or lambskins, upon which were described the **Mysteries of God** <note>original: *Mysteria de Dieu*</note>, the ancient magical secrets, or the high **Hermetic Art** <term>Hermetic Art: The tradition of alchemy, philosophy, and magic based on the writings attributed to the mythical figure Hermes Trismegistus</term> itself, which were preserved by the patriarchs, kings, and priests of all times, and left behind as a trail for their descendants. 

But, as has been said, the foundation of truth

...truth was lacking to them; and nonetheless they saw that it meant something great. For the name, **Alchemy**, was kept as secret by the ancients as the high art itself. Therefore, it could not be held in such ill suspicion, nor could there be so many deceptive writings and starved and twisted **sophists** <note>In alchemical literature, "sophists" often refers to "puffers" or fraudulent practitioners who use false logic and tricks</note>; rather, the art was secret, and known only to the highest of the people.

This led them to the conviction that they possessed the assurance that this word and term is the property of the Alchemists, especially since Suidas <note>The *Suda*, or Suidas, is a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia; it contains an entry claiming the Golden Fleece was actually a book written on parchment that taught how to make gold through alchemy.</note> had already publicly shown, some 600 years before the high Knightly Order was established in Burgundy, that the term *Golden Fleece* <note>original: "Aureum Vellus"</note> belongs to alchemy; and therefore, they could with good reason place such a title at the front of their writings. ## Chapter 2.

...the **Golden Fleece** <note>original: "goldenen Vlies"</note> kept in Colchis, which was retrieved from there by Jason and his company, as described.

**Aloisius Martianus**, a poet and philosopher, wrote a treatise under the name **Golden Fleece** <note>original: "Aureum Vellus"</note>, concerning both alchemy and poetry.

**Aurelius Johannes** <note>Likely Giovanni Aurelio Augurello (c. 1441–1524), an Italian humanist who wrote "Chrysopoeia," a famous poem on making gold that used Jason's quest as a central metaphor.</note>, a famous poet and chemist <note>original: "Chymicus"</note>, who described the history of Jason and his voyage under the title **Golden Fleece** <note>original: "Aureum Vellus"</note>, which he compared with alchemy.

***Golden Fleece*** <note>original: "Aureum Vellus"</note>, a treatise in quarto <note>A book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves, resulting in a medium-sized volume.</note> printed in the year 1598 at Rospach on **Lake Constance** <note>original: "Boden-See"</note>, in which the author treats alchemy and provides considerable grounds for this history.

***Golden Fleece*** <note>original: "Golden-Vlies"</note>, written in octavo <note>A smaller book size where each sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves.</note> in the year 1607, and first printed in 1736 in Leipzig, where the author presents the high **chain of the Order** <note>Referring to the ornate ceremonial collar worn by the Knights of the Order of the Golden Fleece.</note> in a copperplate engraving <note>original: "Kupffer"</note>.

***Golden Fleece*** <note>original: "Aureum Vellus"</note>, a Latin treatise written by **Dr. Criling** <note>Johann Konrad Creiling (1673–1752), a professor at the University of Tübingen who researched both physics and the "Hermetic" arts.</note> in Tübingen, in which alchemy is treated.

## Chapter 3.

### Concerning the First Beginning and Origin of the Well-Known and World-Renowned Term, Golden Fleece.

During the times of the Israelite Judges <note>The leaders of Israel in the era before the establishment of the monarchy, roughly 1200–1000 BCE.</note>, approximately twelve hundred years before the grace-filled birth or incarnation of our dearest Savior and Redeemer Jesus Christ—when He willed to appear in the flesh through the Virgin Mary, from the royal tribe, lineage, and blood of David—but counting from the creation of the world (that great and mighty wonder-structure of Heaven and Earth), it was approximately twenty-seven hundred years later. Already at that time, powerful and mighty peoples and kingdoms existed in the Western <note>original: "Abend-Ländischen"; literally "Evening-lands."</note> and Northern <note>original: "Mitternächtigen"; literally "Midnight-lands."</note> countries, which lay far from the holy land of Canaan; among these, the Greek monarchy is especially to be counted. Thus, in these times, the mighty King and Prince Athamas ruled, who was lord and master of a vast territory called Thebes.

<summary>This page establishes the historical timeline for the origin of the Golden Fleece, dating it to roughly 1200 BCE during the time of the biblical Judges and the reign of King Athamas in Greece.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Fleece, King Athamas, Thebes, chronology, Israelite Judges, Greek history, incarnation</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of King Athamas of Thebes, transitioning from his political status to his domestic life and the character of his wife, Queen Nephele, and their children.</meta> <meta>catchword: of their</meta>

[page-num] 168 [/page-num]

<header>The 3rd Chapter.</header>

was. His consort or queen was Nephele, a very clever lady, by whom the king had two children, namely a prince and a princess; the prince was named Phrixus and the princess Helle <note>The transcription "Höllen" refers to Helle, who famously falls into the sea that would later bear her name, the Hellespont.</note>. King Athamas was a very strict, sharp, jealous, and hot-tempered lord or ruler, after the manner of the Greek nation, before whose person, word, and command everything had to tremble. On the other hand, Nephele was a delicate, timid <note>original: "blöde"; in this historical context, it signifies shyness or modesty rather than a lack of intelligence.</note>, sickly, and fearful person, of a melancholic temperament <note>original: "Melancholischer Complexion"; according to the theory of the four humors, this indicated a personality prone to sadness, introspection, and physical fragility.</note>, who did not harmonize well with her spouse. She could also gather from all circumstances that she would not have long to live, but would die within a few years; whereupon her lord husband and king would marry again and bring a stepmother to these two children who were so close to her heart. She feared he would already possess a step-fatherly heart <note>An archaic expression suggesting that the father’s affection would turn cold or indifferent, mirroring the trope of the "wicked stepmother."</note>, whereby her children would have much to suffer and endure, and might also be poorly raised. ## Chapter 3.

...died and was buried, a memorial pillar was erected and called the **Hellespont** <note>The Hellespont, known today as the Dardanelles, is a narrow strait in northwestern Turkey that connects the Aegean Sea to the Sea of Marmara. It serves as the geographical boundary between Europe and Asia.</note>, which is located near a mountain where there is a very great narrowing, but which has nonetheless become the greatest passage in the world, through and across which one travels from Europe into Asia, or from Asia into Europe, and which is traversed by many thousands of people annually. **Phrixus**, regardless of his great sorrow, continued his journey with all haste so that he might not be betrayed or overtaken. He finally arrived safely at the home of his kinsman, the King of **Colchis** <note>Colchis was an ancient kingdom on the coast of the Black Sea, in present-day Georgia, famously known in myth as the destination of the Argonauts.</note>, where he was received and welcomed with the greatest joy. -># Of the Golden Fleece.<-

<center>M 3</center>

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

**Phrixus**, now King of **Colchis**, has fathered two children with this wife or queen of his, namely a prince and a princess; the prince was given the name **Aetes** <note>original: "Aetha." In classical mythology, he is usually referred to as Aeëtes.</note>, who, after the passing of his father, became King of Colchis. **Phrixus**, however, had made himself exceptionally beloved and held in high esteem through his great knowledge and wisdom: especially when he presented and placed in safekeeping his magnificent book or **golden scroll**—as an honor and an indescribable treasure—in the temple dedicated to **Mars**. This was the place where the royal and national treasures were kept under the supervision of the priesthood, those priests of **Baal** <note>original: "Baals-Pfaffen." A derogatory term for idolatrous priests, here likely used by the author to characterize the guardians of the treasure as greedy or pagan.</note> and servants of **Mammon** <note>A biblical term referring to wealth, often personified as a false god of greed.</note>.

**Aetes** became king in his father's stead after his death and ruled over his kingdom and lands, and had, among other children, a daughter or princess named **Medea**. More on this later.

# Chapter IV.

### How the Golden Fleece at Colchis was recaptured by Jason, the Prince of the King of Thessaly.

After the passage of approximately one century <note>original: "Seculi," a Latin loanword for century.</note> since the previously mentioned history of Phrixus, his sister, and the Golden Fleece, and how he arrived at Colchis with it; and in the first century before the birth of Christ, or in the twenty-eighth century from the creation of the world <note>The author uses a chronology common in early modern texts that dates the world's creation to approximately 4000–3700 BC. ### Chapter V.
#### Of the Poetic Description of the Golden Fleece.

They say: when Athamas, King in Thebes, was to sacrifice his son, Phrixus, and his daughter, Helle, by command of the Oracle <note>original Latin: *Oraculi*</note>, their deceased mother Nephele (who is found among the gods and has been transformed into a cloud) took them into her protection and sent them a ram with golden wool. Upon this ram, they traveled across the Black Sea toward Colchis. Although the daughter, Helle, fell off and drowned—for which reason the place was named the Hellespont—Phrixus nevertheless traveled upon the ram to Colchis. There he sacrificed the ram and hung up the golden skin or fleece in the temple, which afterwards [was sought] by all...

# Chapter 5.

[The fleece was] shown to peoples traveling to Colchis; their desire, however, was to obtain it, yet the king had placed it in such a state of safekeeping that the entrances were guarded by fire-breathing dragons. Afterwards, however, the royal prince from Thessaly, Jason, arrived there and captured it with great cunning, bringing it back to Greece, where he possessed it with great renown.

So far extend the words of the poets concerning the Golden Fleece, which, however, carries the exact same meaning as the historical chronology. Thus we have shown and proven from antiquity how this manner of speaking took its beginning and what was meant by it. Now we are only lacking the philosophical sense, which is where its true meaning <note>original Latin: *sensum*</note> and understanding are properly said to lie.

For Phrixus and Jason were two poets and philosophers who thereby described their own fates and the high Hermetic art <note>The "Hermetic art" refers to alchemy, named after Hermes Trismegistus</note> and science, and

# Of the Golden Fleece.

and under the name, **Golden Fleece**, [they] intended to express and make understood both the **First Matter** <note>original: *Prima Materia*; the primordial substance required to begin the alchemical process</note> as well as the **Practice** <note>original: *Praxin*</note> of the **Philosopher's Stone** itself, as shall be sufficiently shown in its proper place; so that this **term** <note>original: *Terminus*</note> and manner of speaking is an ancient, well-known, and established way of talking, which the poets and philosophers employed, devised for their own use, and incorporated into their writings. From these, later writers subsequently borrowed and derived the same. Here we shall let the matter rest for now, especially since no one is aware of—nor entitled to believe—anything else than that this manner of speaking has been the property of the poets and philosophers since ancient times and belongs to them. The younger and more recent philosophers, who followed the ancients with equal right, have drawn it forth from antiquity and kept it as an inherited possession, making use of it and placing it at the beginning of their writings as a **Hermetic Alphabet** <note>A system of symbolic or encoded language used by followers of Hermes Trismegistus to hide secrets from the uninitiated</note> or introductory greeting, where it shall continue to remain in the future.

## Chapter VII.

### Concerning the Signatures wherewith the High Members of this Noble Order of the Golden Fleece are Signed and Marked.

After this great prince and Duke of Burgundy and Brabant, **Philip III** <note>Historically known as Philip the Good (1396–1467). Therefore, he decreed for them a special and wondrous <term>habit<gloss>The distinctive dress or costume of a religious or knightly order, signaling their status and vows.</gloss></term> or knightly garment, together with a chain of **solid gold** of a form never before seen: so that, if anyone should catch sight of these high knights from afar, they would immediately recognize and respect them, especially since this Order was intended to be something exceptional, having no equal in the world. # Of the Golden Fleece.
N 2
<meta>catchword: bordered</meta>

The Order's chain, or the insignia of the Order, was a chain of solid gold that they were required to wear around the neck, which was formed of eleven links; <note>The number of links in the collar of the Golden Fleece varied over time, but typically consisted of stylized fire-steels and flints.</note> they also wore the same Order’s **habit**, but made of black cloth, and for this, the embroidered hem and the bordering had to be omitted. <note>This black attire was typically worn on the second day of the festival during the Requiem Mass for deceased members.</note>

The third day, with which the three-day festival of the Order and the memorial of **Saint Andrew** ends and is concluded, was celebrated in honor of the holy and highly blessed Mother of God, **Mary**, whom the Duke venerated with special zeal above all others at all times. She was specifically called upon for protection and help on this day; also, a special **habit** and Order’s dress had to be worn in her honor. Namely, on the third day, they had to take off the purple-red tunic and <term>gown<gloss>The original "Talar" refers to a long, formal robe reaching the ankles.</gloss></term> and wear another garment made of white damask.

His son, however, **Duke Charles**, <note>Charles the Bold (reigned 1467–1477)</note> later completely abolished everything that was made of wool or cloth and issued the decree that henceforth on the first day of the Order’s festival, or on **St. Andrew's Day**, instead of the previous clothing of scarlet-red cloth, they should in future times wear hoods and gowns of purple-red velvet, trimmed and lined with white taffeta, and bordered with the wide hem, as before—

...links, each of which represented two <term>fire-steels<gloss>A 'fire-steel' or 'fusil' is a metal tool used with flint to strike sparks; it was the central heraldic motif of the Burgundian Order of the Golden Fleece.</gloss></term>, where in the middle, where the links are joined together, flints are shaped, from which fire-tinder or fire-sparks flash and radiate outward. To look upon such a thing is magnificent. On this chain, there are also two special [pieces]

intended to be worn, upon which the Order’s emblem—namely, the steel and flint-stones spraying sparks and fire, as before—is to be seen embroidered with gold; for this was more the symbol of the Order than of Duke Philip. To this he changed nothing further, except only the single inscription: **I WILL HAVE NO OTHER** <note>original: "AUTRE N'AVRAI"</note> which he altered, and in its place substituted his own motto: **I HAVE UNDERTAKEN IT** <note>original: "JE L'AY EMPRIS" in French and "ILLVD SVSCEPI" in Latin</note>; in German: I have already done this. Which motto, though grave and significant, was yet perfectly suited and appropriate to the Duke’s temperament and spirit.

And since his lord father, Duke Philip, had decreed nothing regarding the other clothing and <term>tunics<gloss>The original "Leib-Röcke" refers to close-fitting body coats or doublets worn under heavier robes.</gloss></term>, and everyone wore them of cloth and colors as they pleased—blue and variegated—Duke Charles henceforth decreed that these too should be worn in purple-red velvet. So far goes the account of **CHIFLETIUS**. <note>original: "SCHIFFLETII", referring to Jules Chiflet (1610–1676), a historian of the Order of the Golden Fleece.</note>

## Chapter 8.
### Concerning the Successor in the Government of the Duchy of Burgundy and Brabant, as well as in the high Knightly Order itself.

When the great Prince and Duke of Burgundy and Brabant, Philip III, passed away at an advanced age, his successor in all his estates was his only son and lawful hereditary prince, **Charles**, called **the Bold** or **the Warlike**, a very noble lord. He also loved law and justice; yet he was of a very fiery and hot-tempered nature, and was not accustomed to forgive or forget his enemies and those who offended him without seeking revenge, but rather—

Consequently, **Archduke Maximilian** of Austria, as the later Emperor, was Duke of Burgundy, Flanders, and Brabant, as well as the Grand Master of the high Knightly Order of the Golden Fleece.

### Of the Golden Fleece.

However, it did not last very long before his Burgundian consort, **Mary**, passed away. She had borne him a son named **Philip**, known also as the Prince of Luxembourg <note>original: "Lützelburg," an archaic German name for Luxembourg</note>, Duke of Burgundy and Brabant, as well as Archduke of Austria. After the death of his father, **Emperor Maximilian**, the lands inherited from his mother, along with this Knightly Order, fell to him. Thus, these Burgundian, Brabantine, and Flemish lands, together with the high Knightly Order of the Golden Fleece, passed to Spain. **Philip** was therefore the First of that name as King of Spain, or the Fourth as Duke of Burgundy and Brabant, as well as Archduke of Austria and Grand Master of the high Knightly Order of the Golden Fleece.

### Chapter 9.

#### The third part of our undertaking.

Concerning proofs that this high Knightly Order of the Golden Fleece has something more at its foundation, or signifies more, than one might perhaps perceive with the naked eye.

After we have already set forth and proven that the word, or the phrase, "the Golden Fleece," is an ancient word and phrase, which took its basis and origin from ancient Heathendom and Greek culture, and was devised and used by the poets, philosophers, and alchemists, as well as by the Magi <note>original: "Magis"; refers here to practitioners of esoteric wisdom or magic</note> and Cabbalists <note>original: "Cabalisten"; followers of Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism often adapted by Christian scholars in this period</note>; and indeed as a customary or inherited phrase, which was their own property, belonging to them alone, and

### Chapter 9.

and belongs to them: It still remains for us to prove and show that this **Duke Philip of Burgundy and Brabant**, as the founder of this high **Order of Knighthood**, must have had far different and indeed essential intentions as his basis and ultimate purpose than merely to found this high Order for the sake of statecraft and prestige; <note>The original German "Staat" here refers to outward pomp, courtly display, and the formal majesty of a prince's household.</note> and that he aimed at something more thorough, which has something substantial at its center, in honor of which he wished to erect a **pyramid** or **memorial column**: so that posterity, like passing pilgrims, might stop there, refresh themselves a little, and be prompted to ask what it portends; just as all **writers** <note>original: "Scribenten"</note> have stood together in the greatest amazement, asking and searching: what could have moved the Duke?

1) To desire to found and establish such a high princely, even royal, Knightly Order, and to bestow upon it an ancient heathen and Greek name, which to all outward appearances seems to contain nothing essential and nothing profound or fundamental.

2) Why he founded this high **Order of Knighthood** on the day of his **wedding**, in honor of his wife, the wedding guests present, the wedding itself, the **Holy Virgin Mary**, and in memory of the **Holy Apostle Andrew**? <note>St. Andrew was the patron saint of the Burgundian lands.</note>

3) Why in honor of the Apostolic Patrons, the first teachers of the Gospel, and at the same time to undertake a war against the **Saracens** <note>A historical term used in the Middle Ages and Early Modern period to refer to Muslims, particularly those in the Mediterranean and Holy Land.</note> or a campaign against the **infidels**?

These three questions or circumstances have made them <note>The "writers" mentioned on the previous page.</note> pause to investigate the cause, as these appear to be contradictory things; as indeed they are in truth. For how can it harmonize and be consistent for a pious Christian to have **Christian works** and heathen concepts; to practice or perform a Christian way of life, Christian intentions, and **Christian works**, under a heathen sense, name, and cover? For if he founded this high Order of Knighthood as a memorial of his wedding, the wedding guests, as well as—

We humans often see things where we find no help, comfort, or counsel, and we believe that if God does not act supernaturally, as it were, then all is lost. Here, however, there was no state of emergency that necessitated God acting in such a supernatural way or allowing such an abominable <term>monster<gloss>The author uses the term 'Monstrum' in the classical sense of a 'prodigy' or a creature that defies the natural order, often seen as a sign or omen.</gloss></term> to be brought forth. How much less, then, outside of His Church, etc.

# Of the Golden Fleece.

“...to conquer and to plunder, etc. It so happened, however, that the Order’s Chancellor, named **JOHANNES GERMANUS** <note>Jean Germain (c. 1400–1461), Bishop of Châlons-sur-Saône and the first Chancellor of the Order of the Golden Fleece.</note>, who was also Bishop of Châlons in Burgundy, a famous and excellent orator, and one of the most distinguished prelates, who stood in particularly high esteem at court, reminded [the Duke] of **Gideon’s fleece** <note>In the biblical Book of Judges, Gideon asks God for a sign of victory by placing a wool fleece on a threshing floor; God makes the fleece wet with dew while the ground remains dry, and vice-versa.</note>, and thereby caused—**NB.** <note>Latin: *nota bene*, meaning "note well."</note> one (not the Duke) to change the previous thoughts, abandon the first design, and in its place accept the Biblical history of Gideon’s fleece as the model and example.” (*)

### Chapter 9.

Therefore, let no one fall into the thinking that in more recent times, perhaps only out of a loss of reputation, a comparison was made between the high Order and **Jason’s** golden fleece; no, not at all. Rather, everyone should be convinced that just as **Duke Philip** <note>Philip the Good (1396–1467), Duke of Burgundy.</note> named and established the high Order of Knighthood of the Golden Fleece, he had his own specific intentions. Likewise, the great **Duke Philip of Burgundy and Brabant** adopted the fable of **Jason** and the **Golden Fleece** because he intended to undertake a journey to the Promised Land as significant as **Jason’s** journey to **Colchis** <note>In Greek mythology, Colchis was the distant land where the Golden Fleece was kept, guarded by a dragon.</note>. If this were the case, then **Germanus** <note>Jean Germain, the Bishop and Chancellor mentioned on the previous page who advocated for the biblical Gideon over the pagan Jason.</note> would not have needed to think of another fleece; instead, the original design would have remained, as indeed it has truly remained. For one finds no record at all that either **Duke Philip** or **Duke Charles** <note>Charles the Bold (1433–1477), Philip’s son and successor.</note> suspended or revoked anything regarding it, even though the latter improved and corrected many things within the Order's statutes. In addition, it should be remembered that **Duke Philip III** established the high Order in the year 1430, yet it was not until the year 1443 in **Brussels** that he took the vow to undertake a maritime expedition to the Promised Land.

The author highlights the thirteen-year gap between the Order's founding (1430) and the Duke's formal crusade vow (1443).</summary>

The Swiss <term>Helvetier</term> <note>The Latinized term for the people of the Swiss Confederacy.</note>, who were the relief force <term>Succurs</term>, learned of this strange and treacherous event. They hurried with long strides that very same day toward Grandson and immediately attacked the Ducal camp in a total fury, amid such zealous prayer, tears, and sighs for vengeance over the great faithlessness, perjury <term>Meyn-Eyd</term>, pride, and malice. ### Chapter 10.

Velvet, damask, pieces of gold-cloth, and similar costly fabrics without number.

There was so much money that it was not even valued, but rather distributed by the hatful. One must also not forget many other things that were lost to time, partly because of the great joy over the victory, but partly due to the grief over the betrayal committed against their own people and those who were killed at Grandson <note>This refers to the Swiss garrison at Grandson whom Charles the Bold had hanged or drowned after they surrendered, an act of "unfaithfulness" that incensed the Swiss Confederacy.</note>.

Furthermore, according to the dear simplicity and honesty of those times, not everything was even recorded in writing. Instead, each group of the allied confederates <term>Bundes Genossen</term> <note>The soldiers of the various Swiss cantons and their allies.</note> wrote into their own record-books <term>Gedächtnis-Register</term> <note>literally "memory-registers"; these were local ledgers or municipal chronicles used to track the events and spoils of the campaign.</note> upon their return home, according to how each man understood it and found it to be true. Consequently, much has still not appeared in the official public chronicles; had it been included, the total sum <term>Facit</term> <note>original: "Facit". A Latin-derived term commonly used in early modern German to denote the final result or "the bottom line" of an account.</note> would have been even greater.

# Of the Golden Fleece.
<meta>catchword: expenditures</meta>

When the Archduke of Austria <note>Maximilian I (1459–1519), who married Mary of Burgundy in 1477</note> intended to hold his wedding in Flanders, the Emperor <note>Frederick III, Maximilian’s father</note> had his son dressed—though sparingly, yet as is easy to imagine, still expensively—along with his entire retinue. However, it was brought to the bride's attention that this could have been done with even greater expense; so she sent 100,000 Guilders <term>Gl.</term> <note>original: "Gl."; Guilders or Florins, standard gold currency</note> to meet her bridegroom, so that he might outfit himself more magnificently. Now, if such a sum could be so easily spared, is it not easy to judge that even greater treasures lay in those coffers? When one considers all these reflections, who would not fall into the thought that this great Duke <note>Charles the Bold, Mary's father, or the Burgundian line in general</note> must have drawn his so indescribable riches from somewhere else than having taken them from his subjects? We say, who would not fall into the thought that such revenues and great treasures must have flowed from elsewhere? For whoever has looked even a little into the revenue chambers <term>Renth-Kammern</term> <note>original: "Renth-Kammern"; the administrative offices responsible for managing a ruler's finances and domains</note> of the courts, and knows what the revenues and expenditures of the same amount to, will admit that even a courtly household <term>Hofhaltung</term> that is not conducted with too much pomp regarding the domestic staff <term>Domestiquen</term> <note>original: "Domestiquen"; the servants and attendants of a noble household</note> requires great expenditures

# Chapter 10.

...expenditures require; let alone such a Royal courtly household <term>Hofhaltung</term> <note>original: "Hofhaltung"; the entire administrative, domestic, and ceremonial establishment of a prince</note>, where many thousands of noblemen <term>Edelleute</term> and servants are present, where an open table <note>the practice of providing daily meals at the ruler's expense for courtiers and guests</note> is kept daily, and a more than Royal state is maintained; such things also require the financial resources <note>original: "Interessen"; referring here to the vast revenues or capital interests</note> of mighty kings. How then should it be possible that a Duke could sustain such things, and additionally maintain an army—which at times consisted of 100,000 men—for nearly fifty years, yet at the same time enrich his treasuries <term>Schatz-Kammern</term> and expand his territories with money, without ruining himself and his entire country?

One sees in the most powerful potentates <term>Potentaten</term> <note>sovereign monarchs or rulers with great power</note> of our times that when they have twenty to thirty years of peace <term>Frieden</term>, they fill their chambers with money and acquire a great supply; and yet, if they fall into war <term>Krieg</term>, they are not able to wage it for even two or three years before their supply-chambers and coffers are emptied again, so that they must burden their subjects with all kinds of new levies <term>Auflagen</term> <note>taxes or extraordinary financial demands imposed by a ruler</note>, whereby the land is sucked dry, impoverished, and placed under a burden of debt <term>Schulden-Last</term>.

<summary>The author argues that the Duke of Burgundy’s ability to fund a massive military and a lavish court for fifty years without taxing his subjects into poverty suggests a source of wealth beyond traditional taxation.</summary>

# Chapter 10.

...thought, that the great Duke founded the **Order of the Golden Fleece** on his wedding day, in honor of the **Virgin Mary** and the **Holy Apostle Andrew**, under the pretext of launching a campaign against the **Saracens** <note>A medieval term used to refer to Muslims, particularly during the Crusades</note>. The honored reader is requested to compare <note>original: "conferiren"</note> these points against one another; then there will be no doubt that he will find not the slightest agreement between them.

For 1.) it is well known that not all guests who were present at the wedding were knighted. 

2.) Others, who were not at the wedding, nevertheless became knights. Consequently, the order was not created in honor of the wedding guests. 

3.) One has always been content to choose only one patron, and not two; as the Scripture says: **No one can serve two masters.** <note>A reference to Matthew 6:24</note>

4.) Under the pretext of undertaking a campaign against the Saracens—and yet, note well <note>original: "NB." for *nota bene*</note>, not everyone who participated in these campaigns was made a knight, as they were only 24 to 30 in number. 

Consequently, the name was indeed chosen in honor of the term borrowed from the **Chemists** <term>Chemists: In this context, the author refers to Alchemists, who used the "Golden Fleece" as a symbol for the perfection of matter or the Philosopher's Stone</term>: the **Golden Fleece**.

## The sixth proof-reason is:

The **habit** or **Order-dress**, which the high-ranking knights wear, and particularly on the high feast day: on the first day—as **Chiffletius**, the Order’s Grand Chancellor, and **Mening** testify—it is made of **velvet** and is **scarlet-red**; on the second day, **black**; and on the third day, **white**. Through these colors, the astonishing secrets of the **Great Art** <term>Great Art: A traditional name for Alchemy, referring to the process of transmuting base metals into gold or attaining spiritual perfection</term> are indicated. <note>In alchemical tradition, the colors black (Nigredo), white (Albedo), and red (Rubedo) represent the three primary stages of the "Great Work."</note>

## The seventh proof-reason is:

Furthermore, the **habit** or **Order-dress**, according to the reports of the first Chancellor of the Order, **Germanici**, and another Chancellor of the Order, **Chiffletius**, is said to have consisted of a **talar** <term>talar: A long, formal ceremonial gown or robe reaching the ankles</term> of **scarlet-red** cloth, trimmed and lined with delicate **fur** <note>original: "Rauchwerck," referring to dressed skins or furs</note>. On the outside, this gown had a wide hem featuring the Duke’s **emblem** <note>original: "Sinn-Bild"</note>—namely, **fire-steel** and **fire-sparks**—embroidered in gold, along with a **motto** <note>original: "Devise"</note> of the Duke: **I shall have no other** <note>original French: "AUTRE N'AURAI"</note> or: **I have no other** <note>original Latin: "NON HABEO ALIVD"</note>.

### The eighth ground of proof is:

The golden chain of the Order, which these noble Knights of the Order wore, was a <term>fire-starter <note>original German: "Feuerzeug." In the context of the Order of the Golden Fleece, this refers specifically to the *fusil* (a steel B-shaped striking tool) and the flints that make up the links of the collar.</note></term>—that is, a fire-steel and flint stone, and sparks of fire spraying from them. This unusual style and form of the chain must be based on something special, since it can be compared neither with the Golden Fleece itself, nor with the campaign against the Saracens, nor with the affairs of the wedding. <note>The author is refuting the common historical explanations for the Order's symbols—such as the Duke of Burgundy's marriage or a planned Crusade—suggesting instead a more ancient, esoteric source.</note>

History reports that the great Persian prophet, king, and lawgiver, **Zoroaster**, left behind a law given to his people, along with his great sciences and arts, engraved in a book of copper plates in the shape of a fire-starter—consisting of fire-steel and flints, with spraying sparks of fire—which bears the name: *Zent*, or *Zenta vesta* <note>original: "Zenta vesta." This is a phonetic rendering of the *Zend-Avesta*, the primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism.</note>, that is, a **Law of the Fire-Art** <note>The author interprets Zoroastrianism through an alchemical lens, equating the "sacred fire" of the Persians with the "fire-art" of alchemy.</note>. This Zoroaster, according to many opinions, lived as early as the time of Abraham and was called "Hermes the Great" (if it was not Abraham himself), who, after the Great Flood,

...[he] was the first who is said to have found the iron and copper plates of the arts of the seven sages of the ancient world, engraved by the master **Tubal-cain** <note>Tubal-cain is described in Genesis 4:22 as the first "forger of all instruments of bronze and iron." These tablets, however, were afterwards translated into the **Arabic language** by this **Zoroaster** and written into the aforementioned book. The content of this book is said to be identical to its title and its physical form, which takes the shape of a <term>fire-starter <note>original German: "Feuerzeug." This book is said to remain even to this day in the custody of the **Persian priests**, where it is kept as a great and ancient sanctuary. This great Prince <note>Referring to the founder of the Order, Duke Philip the Good of Burgundy.</note> was presumably informed of this book, and for that reason, he had the chain of his Knightly Order made exactly according to the model of the *Zenta vesta* <note>original: "Zenta vesta," a phonetic spelling of the *Zend-Avesta*, the primary collection of religious texts in Zoroastrianism.</note>. Upon every link of these chains, one can see the "book"—that is, two fire-steels standing together against a flint stone, complete with rays of fire and sparks spraying forth. # Of the Golden Fleece.

In this 8th point of evidence, a supporting ground must also be included: the **Golden Fleece** or **lambskin**, which hangs at the very bottom of the chain. This is the symbol of the Greek princes, **Phrixus** and **Jason**, of the **Golden Ram**, or—according to the custom of the ancients—the scroll made from ram and lambskins. Upon this scroll, the <term>Art of Gold-Generation <note>original German: "Gold-Erzeugungs-Kunst." This refers to alchemical transmutation, the process of creating gold from base materials.</note></term>, along with other sublime sciences, was written in golden colors. Thus, this great Prince <note>Referring to Duke Philip the Good of Burgundy, who founded the Order of the Golden Fleece in 1430.</note>, in creating this knightly collar, looked toward two ancient passages handed down from antiquity, founded his work upon them, and adopted them as a teaching; this certainly deserves to be noted.

### Chapter 10.

...thought-provoking words that are written around this collar of the Order, surrounding the lamb or **Golden Fleece** as a motto, namely: **THE REWARD FOR LABOR IS NOT SMALL.** <note>original: "PRETIUM NON VILE LABORIS." This is the official motto of the Order of the Golden Fleece. While it traditionally refers to the rewards of knightly service, the author here interprets it through an alchemical lens, suggesting the "Great Work" of transmutation.</note> 

It is as if the great Prince and **Duke Philip**, or rather this golden collar of the Order itself, wished to speak and call out to those passing by: "Dear people who gaze upon me, look at my praiseworthy and wisdom-filled form, and contemplate it well. For I ask you, take the trouble and stand still here a while; cast off all distracting thoughts. Instead, double your attention, grasp me with a deep-seeking mind, and search with great desire for the basis of my **presentation** <note>In this context, "presentation" refers to the symbolic way the Order's regalia represents deeper truths.</note>, for I am worthy of you showing me this honor. 

Yes, I tell you, exert all your strength, senses, and desires to contemplate me in both my **centers**—that of the outer and the inner essence. For first of all, the gold—the metallic **lump of gold** which you see here with your eyes—is not the kind of gold that is hewn out of the mountains of the great world,
